People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess what brought me in is I’m losing a lot of time rechecking things. Like, my dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—stuff like that. I’ll try to move on, but it feels like I’m not really allowed to until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.

And it sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I’m an engineer, so checking details is kind of part of the job, you know? Like dimensions and revisions matter. But this is different. It’s not just being careful—it turns into hours sometimes, and then I’m late or I can’t focus on the next thing because my mind is still stuck on whether I checked it “right.”

It’s… yeah, it’s starting to mess things up more than I wanted to admit. At work especially, my project deadlines have started slipping because I’ll stay late trying to get comfortable enough to actually submit something. Like I’ll be there after everyone’s gone, still checking a file or notes, and then I tell myself it’s just being thorough—but it’s not really efficient anymore.

And outside work, I’ve stopped making it to paddleboarding meetups, which is annoying because that used to be kind of my reset. Same with workouts. I’ll think, “I’ll just do the routine and then go,” but then the checking eats up the time I had set aside, and by the time I’m done it’s too late or I’m too drained. So… yeah, it’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.

In the moment, it kind of feels justified… like I know that sounds weird, but if I’m checking dressing supplies or a door lock or file dimensions, my brain is saying, “No, this is important, you can’t move on yet.”

Then later I can see it’s excessive, because I’ve lost hours to it. Like I’ll have gone through the same routine again and again just waiting for the anxiety to drop enough. It’s almost like I’m not allowed to stop until it feels right, even if part of me knows I already checked.

I’m not totally sure. I mean, I worry about work, but it usually hooks onto a specific thing I’m checking, if that makes sense. Like a drawing or a measurement or whether I did something right.

Like I’ll be there after everyone’s gone, still checking a file or notes, and then I tell myself it’s just being thorough—but it’s not really efficient anymore.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped making it to paddleboarding meetups, which is annoying because that used to be kind of my reset. Same with workouts. I’ll think, “I’ll just do the routine and then go,” but then the checking eats up the time I had set aside, and by the time I’m done it’s too late or I’m too drained. So… yeah, it’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck rechecking, in that moment do you feel like the level of checking you’re doing is clearly excessive or unreasonable, or does it mostly feel justified to you?\n\nPatient: In the moment, it kind of feels justified… like I know that sounds weird, but if I’m checking dressing supplies or a door lock or file dimensions, my brain is saying, “No, this is important, you can’t move on yet.”\n\nThen later I can see it’s excessive, because I’ve lost hours to it. Like I’ll have gone through the same routine again and again just waiting for the anxiety to drop enough. It’s almost like I’m not allowed to stop until it feels right, even if part of me knows I already checked.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these specific situations where you’re rechecking things, do you also find yourself worrying a lot about many different everyday things—like money, health, work performance, or family—most days, even when nothing in particular has gone wrong?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I worry about work, but it usually hooks onto a specific thing I’m checking, if that makes sense. Like a drawing or a measurement or whether I did something right.\n\nMoney or family stuff… I don’t think I’m sitting around worrying about all of that most days. Or, well, sometimes I’ll think about money because rent is ridiculous, and then I start thinking about how my friend somehow found a cheaper place across town, but that’s not really the same thing, I guess.\n\nSo… I don’t think it’s a broad, everything-all-the-time kind of worry. It’s more tied to those specific situations.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Um… not really in the sense of, like, repeating a phrase in my head or doing numbers a certain way. It’s more physical, I guess, or routine-based.

It’s more tied to those specific situations.\n\nDoctor: Apart from physically rechecking things, do you also find yourself doing mental rituals—like repeatedly going over events, numbers, or phrases in your head in a very specific way—to try to feel less anxious or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um… not really in the sense of, like, repeating a phrase in my head or doing numbers a certain way. And sometimes I’ll be thinking through, like, “Did I check that? Did I miss that?” but it’s usually tied to the checking itself, not some separate mental ritual.\n\nSorry, I’m probably overexplaining. It’s just… it’s the repeating and rechecking that gets me stuck.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been having these rechecking routines and feeling like you can’t move on until they feel “right”?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. It’s been long enough that it’s not just a bad week or something. I guess I noticed it most because I started losing actual hours to it—like with dressing supplies, or checking the door lock, or going back through file dimensions and revision notes at work.

What has brought you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brought me in is I’m losing a lot of time rechecking things. Like, my dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—stuff like that. I’ll try to move on, but it feels like I’m not really allowed to until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nAnd it sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I’m an engineer, so checking details is kind of part of the job, you know? Like dimensions and revisions matter. But this is different. It’s not just being careful—it turns into hours sometimes, and then I’m late or I can’t focus on the next thing because my mind is still stuck on whether I checked it “right.”\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to recheck things, is it because you get specific, intrusive thoughts or images that something bad will happen if you don’t do it a certain way, even though you know they don’t really make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s pretty much it. It’s like even after I know I cleaned the wound area, or I know I reviewed the CAD file, this thought cuts back in—like, what if I missed one tiny thing? And then it jumps to, you know, the wound gets infected, or there’s some design mistake that could hurt someone.\n\nAnd I can tell myself, “No, I already checked that,” but it doesn’t really stick. I mean, at work, missing a detail can matter, so it’s not totally out of nowhere, I guess… but this feels different. Like my brain won’t accept the answer, even when I know I’ve done it.\n\nDoctor: Because of this rechecking and the anxiety around it, how much is it actually interfering with your day-to-day life—for example, making you late, hurting your work, or affecting your relationships?\n\nPatient: It’s… yeah, it’s starting to mess things up more than I wanted to admit. At work especially, my project deadlines have started slipping because I’ll stay late trying to get comfortable enough to actually submit something. Like I’ll be there after everyone’s gone, still checking a file or notes, and then I tell myself it’s just being thorough—but it’s not really efficient anymore.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped making it to paddleboarding meetups, which is annoying because that used to be kind of my reset. Same with workouts. I’ll think, “I’ll just do the routine and then go,” but then the checking eats up the time I had set aside, and by the time I’m done it’s too late or I’m too drained. So… yeah, it’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck rechecking, in that moment do you feel like the level of checking you’re doing is clearly excessive or unreasonable, or does it mostly feel justified to you?\n\nPatient: In the moment, it kind of feels justified… like I know that sounds weird, but if I’m checking dressing supplies or a door lock or file dimensions, my brain is saying, “No, this is important, you can’t move on yet.”\n\nThen later I can see it’s excessive, because I’ve lost hours to it. I mean, I worry about work, but it usually hooks onto a specific thing I’m checking, if that makes sense. Like a drawing or a measurement or whether I did something right.\n\nMoney or family stuff… I don’t think I’m sitting around worrying about all of that most days. Or, well, sometimes I’ll think about money because rent is ridiculous, and then I start thinking about how my friend somehow found a cheaper place across town, but that’s not really the same thing, I guess.\n\nSo… I don’t think it’s a broad, everything-all-the-time kind of worry. It’s more tied to those specific situations.\n\nDoctor: Apart from physically rechecking things, do you also find yourself doing mental rituals—like repeatedly going over events, numbers, or phrases in your head in a very specific way—to try to feel less anxious or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um… not really in the sense of, like, repeating a phrase in my head or doing numbers a certain way. It’s more physical, I guess, or routine-based.\n\nLike what brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking actual things—my dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. It’s not just being careful—it turns into hours sometimes, and then I’m late or I can’t focus on the next thing because my mind is still stuck on whether I checked it “right.”\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to recheck things, is it because you get specific, intrusive thoughts or images that something bad will happen if you don’t do it a certain way, even though you know they don’t really make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s pretty much it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that’s pretty much what happens. I’ll decide, like, okay, I’m only going to check the door once, or I already looked at the dressing supplies, and then I’ll try to walk away—but it doesn’t feel finished.

It’s like this pressure builds, like I’m not allowed to move on yet. And then I start thinking about whether I missed something, and I end up going back into the same routine. Same with work stuff too, like file dimensions or revision notes—I know that sounds boring, but I can lose so much time because I keep needing to recheck until the anxiety comes down.

Sorry, I started thinking about work there. But yeah, if I cut it short, it gets really uncomfortable fast, and checking again feels like the only way to make it settle.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How long have you been having these rechecking routines and feeling like you can’t move on until they feel “right”?\n\nPatient: It’s been long enough that it’s not just a bad week or something. I guess I noticed it most because I started losing actual hours to it—like with dressing supplies, or checking the door lock, or going back through file dimensions and revision notes at work.\n\nAnd it’s weird because I’ll think, okay, I checked it, but then I still can’t move on until I’ve gone through the routine again and again and the anxiety drops. Sorry, that doesn’t exactly answer how long. I’m not great at pinning down the start. It kind of built up gradually, I think.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, would you say these rechecking routines have been going on for at least a year, or has it been less than a year so far?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, it’s been going on a while. I don’t know if I can put an exact date on it, but it’s definitely not just the last couple months.\n\nWhat made me finally pay attention is that I’m losing hours to it now—like rechecking my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes at work. I’ll tell myself I already checked, but I still don’t feel like I can move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times and the anxiety comes down.\n\nSo, I guess… at least long enough that it’s become a real pattern. I’m sorry, I know you asked year or less than a year—I’m leaning toward at least a year, but I’m not great at marking when it started.\n\nDoctor: When you try to resist or cut short the checking routines—for example, deciding you will check something only once and then stop—does your anxiety or discomfort quickly build up to the point that it feels almost impossible not to go back and check again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much what happens. I’ll decide, like, okay, I’m only going to check the door once, or I already looked at the dressing supplies, and then I’ll try to walk away—but it doesn’t feel finished.\n\nIt’s like this pressure builds, like I’m not allowed to move on yet. And then I start thinking about whether I missed something, and I end up going back into the same routine. Same with work stuff too, like file dimensions or revision notes—I know that sounds boring, but I can lose so much time because I keep needing to recheck until the anxiety comes down.\n\nSorry, I started thinking about work there. But yeah, if I cut it short, it gets really uncomfortable fast, and checking again feels like the only way to make it settle.\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of the moments when you’re actively checking things, do you ever get sudden, unwanted thoughts or images about something terrible happening that pop into your mind and feel intrusive, like they don’t fit with what you actually want or believe?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 25 / engineer / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"Even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area or reviewed a CAD file, a thought keeps cutting in that I missed one tiny thing and it’s going to turn into an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone, and I can’t just reason it away.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. It’s not just when I’m standing there checking. Like even after I know I cleaned the wound area, or I’ve already reviewed a CAD file, this thought just cuts in like, what if you missed one tiny thing?

And then it sort of runs with it—like the wound could get infected, or the design mistake could hurt someone. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because at work there are reviews and standards and, you know, it’s not like one dimension should become this whole disaster in my head. But the thought doesn’t really care about that.

It feels intrusive, yeah. Like I don’t want it there, and I can’t just reason it away by telling myself I already checked.

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S066\",\"name\":\"Compulsions\",\"description\":\"Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.\",\"reason\":\"The question is asking about the duration of the rechecking routines, which are the compulsive behaviors described in this profile.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger.
